A unified proof of sharp bounds for the Jacobi heat kernel with trace and estimates of multiplicative constants Thanks: The second-named author was supported by the Foundation for Polish Science (START 057.2023). The third-named author was supported by the Simons Foundation grant SFI-MPS-TSM-00013714 and by the National Science Centre, Poland, grant Sonata Bis 2022/46/E/ST1/00036.
Adam Nowak, Paweł Plewa, Tomasz Z. Szarek

Abstract
. We give a unified and optimized proof of the sharp bounds for the Jacobi heat kernel, which were obtained gradually in several papers in recent years. We lay particular emphasis on tracing and estimating all constants appearing throughout the entire reasoning. This allows us to quantitatively control the multiplicative constants in the Jacobi heat kernel bounds in terms of the parameters involved. Consequently, analogous control extends to a number of interrelated heat kernels. In particular, we obtain quantitative control in terms of the associated dimension for the spherical heat kernel and for all other heat kernels on compact rank one symmetric spaces.



†
†

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification:

Primary 35K08; Secondary 42C05, 58J35, 58J65, 60J65.

Key words and phrases:

Jacobi heat kernel, spherical heat kernel, CROSS heat kernel, sharp estimate, Jacobi diffusion process, spherical Brownian motion,
CROSS Brownian motion.

1.
Introduction

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
. The
Jacobi heat kernel
associated with the parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
is given by
1
1

1

The numbers
n(n+\alpha+\beta+1)
occurring in the exponential factors are the eigenvalues of the Jacobi Laplacian.

G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-tn(n+\alpha+\beta+1)}\;\frac{P_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}(x)P_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}(y)}{h_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}},\qquad x,y\in[-1,1],\quad t>0.

Here,
P_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}
are the classical
Jacobi polynomials
(cf.
[
38
]
), and

h_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}:=\|P_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(d\varrho_{\alpha,\beta})}
with the Jacobi orthogonality measure on
[-1,1]

{\rm d}\varrho_{\alpha,\beta}(x)=(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}\,{\rm d}x.

The normalizing constants have the explicit form

h_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{2^{\alpha+\beta+1}\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)\Gamma(n+\beta+1)}{(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+1)\Gamma(n+1)},

where for
n=0
and
\alpha+\beta=-1
the product
(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)\Gamma(n+\alpha+\beta+1)

is interpreted as
1
.
Note that
h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}=\varrho_{\alpha,\beta}([-1,1])
.

The Jacobi heat kernel is an important object in analysis, probability, physics, and other areas.
The kernel
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
provides solution to the heat equation based on the
Jacobi Laplacian

J^{\alpha,\beta}=-\big(1-x^{2}\big)\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}x^{2}}-\big[\beta-\alpha-(\alpha+\beta+2)x\big]\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}x}.

From a probabilistic perspective,
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
is the transition probability density for the
Jacobi diffusion process
,
which has attracted considerable attention due to its utility in stochastic modeling
in physics, neuroscience, genetics, environmental science, and economics, among others; see the Internet.
Investigations related to the behavior of the Jacobi heat kernel have deep theoretical and practical motivations.
In particular, it subordinates in a sense a number of other more elementary heat kernels that are important and of interest.
One of the most prominent examples here is the
spherical heat kernel
.

For
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
t>0
define

Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi):=\big[t+\theta\varphi\big]^{-\alpha-1/2}\big[t+(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)\big]^{-\beta-1/2}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{(\theta-\varphi)^{2}}{4t}\bigg).

The following sharp estimates were proved in a series of papers by Nowak, Sjögren and Szarek.

Theorem 1

(
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
and
T>0
be fixed. There exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\beta,T)>1
such that

C^{-1}\cdot Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi)\leq G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)\leq C\cdot Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi)

for
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
0<t\leq T
.

A direct consequence of the above, see e.g.,
[
25
, p. 233]
or Section
5
below, are sharp large time estimates

C^{-1}\leq G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\leq C,\qquad x,y\in[-1,1],\quad t\geq T,

for any
\alpha,\beta>-1
and
T>0
, with some constant
C=C(\alpha,\beta,T)>1
independent of
x,y,t
.

A major deficiency of the above estimates is the complete lack of information about the sizes of the
constants. The main objective of this paper is to address this gap, which is crucial for various applications.
Our secondary objective is to optimize and unify the entire existing proof of the sharp bounds,
which is scattered in
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
.

One should emphasize that the oscillatory series defining the Jacobi heat kernel cannot be computed explicitly.
However, it is known that in the four simple cases
\alpha,\beta=\pm 1/2
the kernel can be written by means of
non-oscillating series as follows.
2
2

2

The argument is based on simple initial-value problems for the classical heat equation on an interval with
Neumann/Dirichlet boundary conditions. Moreover, the formulas for
\mathbb{G}_{t}^{-1/2,-1/2}(\theta,\varphi)
and

\mathbb{G}_{t}^{1/2,1/2}(\theta,\varphi)
are, in principle, well-known Jacobi type identities, see
[
12
, Sec. 1.8.4]
.

Let

\vartheta_{t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}\,\exp\bigg(-\frac{(z+2\pi n)^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad t>0,

be the periodized Gauss-Weierstrass kernel.
3
3

3

The function
\vartheta_{t}
is essentially one of the Jacobi theta functions, which has deep
connections with elliptic functions and number theory. In the notation of
the Bateman Manuscript Project
[
1
, Vol. 2, Sec. 13.19]
, one has

\vartheta_{t}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\theta_{3}(\frac{z}{2\pi}|\frac{it}{\pi})

(see e.g.,
[
12
, Sec. 1.7.5]
for the relevant identity).

Further, denote

\mathbb{G}_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\theta,\varphi):=2^{\alpha+\beta+1}e^{-t(\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2})^{2}}\Big(\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)^{\alpha+1/2}\Big(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)^{\beta+1/2}G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi);

this is the heat kernel associated with Jacobi function expansions, see e.g.,
[
25
, Sec. 2]
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{t}^{-1/2,-1/2}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{t}(\theta-\varphi)+\vartheta_{t}(\theta+\varphi),

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{t}^{1/2,1/2}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{t}(\theta-\varphi)-\vartheta_{t}(\theta+\varphi),

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{t}^{-1/2,1/2}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}-\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)+\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}+\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)-\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}-\frac{\varphi}{2}+\pi\Big)-\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}+\frac{\varphi}{2}+\pi\Big)\bigg],

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{t}^{1/2,-1/2}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}-\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)-\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}+\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)-\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}-\frac{\varphi}{2}+\pi\Big)+\vartheta_{t/4}\Big(\frac{\theta}{2}+\frac{\varphi}{2}+\pi\Big)\bigg].

Accordingly, one obtains analogous formulas for
G_{t}^{\pm 1/2,\pm 1/2}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)
,
with a limiting interpretation in certain cases.
These formulas allow one to describe the behavior in a direct,
though technically sophisticated, way.
Nevertheless, our approach in this paper is different and oriented toward general
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Historical background

Special instances of the Jacobi heat kernel have existed (at least implicitly) in the literature since the 19th century,
and the question of describing its behavior has been a long-standing open problem.
Strict positivity of
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
was shown by Karlin and McGregor
[
17
]
in 1960.
Slightly earlier, Bochner
[
2
]
proved that the ultraspherical heat kernel
G_{t}^{\alpha,\alpha}(x,y)
is non-negative when
\alpha\geq-1/2
.
Some later results on the positivity, from the 1970s, can be found in Gasper
[
13
]
and Bochner
[
3
]
.

Apart from the positivity, the behavior of the Jacobi heat kernel has not been effectively investigated until quite recently.
Qualitatively sharp bounds for
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
were obtained by Coulhon, Kerkyacharian and Petrushev
[
7
]
, using the theory of Dirichlet forms and other abstract tools. Independently, and with a completely different analytic approach,
qualitatively sharp estimates were proved by Nowak and Sjögren
[
25
]
, under the restriction
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
.

The difference between (genuinely) sharp and qualitatively sharp estimates is that, in the latter case, the constants
appearing in the exponential factors in the lower and upper bounds (which are
1/4
in Theorem
1
) differ
from each other and from the optimal one. This exponential gap absorbs various polynomial factors and therefore
significantly affects the precision of the bounds.

Sharp estimates for
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
, see Theorem
1
, were derived gradually in
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
.
More precisely, in
[
27
]
, the result was obtained when
\alpha=\beta=d/2-1
,
d\geq 1
, and with
y=1
the endpoint.
Then in
[
28
]
all parameters
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
were admitted and the constraint on
y
removed.
Finally, the most difficult case when
\alpha
or
\beta
is below
-1/2
was recently dealt with in
[
29
]
.

Description of present results

We give a unified and optimized proof of the sharp bounds stated in Theorem
1
.
Comparing to the known reasoning
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
, in the unified proof
we avoid many intermediate estimates that deal with the most delicate situation when the arguments of the heat kernel
lie at opposite endpoints of
[-1,1]
. Moreover, we bypass multiple iterations of certain intermediate steps what
leads to more optimal multiplicative constants.

Following the main objective of this paper, we make all the relevant constants explicit in the reasoning, so that
multiplicative constants in the Jacobi heat kernel bounds can be directly traced back through the whole proof
and, consequently, explicitly controlled in terms of the parameters and the time threshold.
This requires overcoming numerous conceptual and technical difficulties.

The unified proof has a deliberate block structure, consisting of Steps A to F, presented in Section
4
.
These Steps form autonomous modules, which can be independently analyzed and possibly refined separately in the future.
The whole proof combines many facts/tools/results related to the Jacobi framework and also involves other auxiliary technical results.
Perhaps the most crucial device underlying the presented analysis is a product formula for Jacobi polynomials due to
Dijksma and Koornwinder
[
11
]
.
It is worth noting that aspects of the theory of special functions and combinatorics are also involved in the reasoning.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
. For
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
,
t>0
, and a constant
\kappa>0
define

\Xi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\kappa}(t;\theta,\varphi):=\big[\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}t\vee\kappa\theta\varphi\big]^{-\alpha-1/2}\big[\mathbb{D}_{\beta}t\vee\kappa(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)\big]^{-\beta-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{(\theta-\varphi)^{2}}{4t}\bigg),

where
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}=\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)^{1/(\alpha+1/2)}
with the limiting understanding when
\alpha=-1/2

(see Section
2.5
), and
\vee
denotes the maximum of two quantities.
The constant
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
depends approximately linearly on
\alpha
, see Remark
2.12
.
With
\kappa>0
and
\alpha,\beta>-1
fixed, the quantity
\Xi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\kappa}(t;\theta,\varphi)
is explicitly comparable
with
Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi)
, uniformly in
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
t>0
.
It turns out that
\Xi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\kappa}(t;\theta,\varphi)
is better suited
to our approach for describing the behavior of the Jacobi heat kernel.
In particular, it allows us to formulate more accurate estimates than the analogous ones based on
Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi)
.

We now state a principal result that
follows from the unified proof. See Lemma
F
in Section
4
and Proposition

6.2
together with (
6.13
) and Propositions
6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
in Section
6.3
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
and
T>0
be fixed. There exist positive constants
C_{\alpha,\beta,T}
and
c_{\alpha,\beta,T}
such that

c_{\alpha,\beta,T}\cdot\Xi^{\alpha,\beta}_{1/2}(t;\theta,\varphi)\leq G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)\leq C_{\alpha,\beta,T}\cdot\Xi^{\alpha,\beta}_{2/\pi^{2}}(t;\theta,\varphi)

for
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
0<t\leq T
.
Moreover, the multiplicative constants satisfy the following bounds, given the indicated time thresholds.

(i)

For
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
such that
\alpha+\beta+1\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{11\cdot(18/5)^{\alpha+\beta+1}}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad T>0,

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}(11/3)\cdot(17/5)^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=2/(\alpha+\beta+3/2)\\
16\cdot(8/5)^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=1/(\alpha+\beta+3/2)^{2}\end{cases}.

(ii)

For
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
such that
\alpha+\beta+1/2\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{14\cdot 31^{\alpha+\beta+1}}\qquad\textrm{if}\quad T=4/(\alpha+\beta+5/4),

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}11\cdot 23^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=4/(\alpha+\beta+5/4)\\
45\cdot 5^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=1/(\alpha+\beta+5/4)^{2}\end{cases}.

(iii)

For
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
such that
\alpha+\beta>-1/2
and
2\alpha+2\beta+1\notin\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{318\cdot 31^{\alpha+\beta+1}}\qquad\textrm{if}\quad T=4/(\alpha+\beta+7/4),

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}28\cdot 23^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=4/(\alpha+\beta+7/4)\\
124\cdot 5^{\alpha+\beta+1}&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=1/(\alpha+\beta+7/4)^{2}\end{cases}.

(iv)

For
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
such that
-1<\alpha+\beta<-1/2
,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8\cdot 10^{6}}\qquad\textrm{if}\quad T=16/11,

\displaystyle C_{\alpha,\beta,T}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}20107&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=16/11\\
1189&\quad\textrm{if}\quad T=16/121\end{cases}.

Items (i)–(iv) of Theorem
2
cover all parameters
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
.
From the unified proof, analogous estimates can be obtained for the remaining values of the parameters. This requires more effort, but
is merely a matter of lengthy technical elaboration. Since the case when one of the parameters is below
-1/2
is not
crucial for applications, we leave the details to interested readers.

We believe that the ratio of the upper and lower multiplicative constants in
Theorem
2
must grow no slower than exponentially with
\alpha
and
\beta
.
This is due to a certain incompatibility between the bounds’ structure and the actual behavior of the kernel.
Estimates for the ratio in the case
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
can be found in Propositions

6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
in Section
6.3
.
Those bounds grow exponentially in
\alpha
and
\beta
.
On the other hand, no worse than only exponential loss was intended by us and, in fact, it required quite a lot of effort
to obtain it comparing to e.g. a loss of order of magnitude given by the gamma function.
The optimal choice of the structure and shape of the expression describing the behavior of
the kernel is, no doubt, a tricky question, beyond the scope of this paper.

Theorem
2
deals essentially with small times. But this also covers medium and large times in a well-known direct way,
see Section
5
. Indeed, if
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
is bounded below and above by two positive constants at some

t=t_{0}>0
, independently of
x,y\in[-1,1]
, then it remains bounded by the same constants for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

On the other hand, it is known that, see e.g.,
[
25
, Thm. A]
,

G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+2)}{2^{\alpha+\beta+1}\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\beta+1)},\qquad t\to\infty,

and the convergence is uniform in
x,y\in[-1,1]
.
Thus, for large
t
, even sharp estimates of the kernel are unsatisfactory, since
they neither detect nor quantify the convergence.
Therefore, we present an additional result, see Theorem
5.2
, which makes the convergence quantitative in
terms of suitable estimates. In the statement below
\wedge
denotes the minimum of two quantities.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
. Then for
x,y\in[-1,1]
and
t\geq 2\log 2

\bigg|G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)-\frac{1}{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}\bigg|\leq\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+2)}{2^{\alpha+\beta}\Gamma(\alpha\wedge\beta+2)\Gamma(\alpha\vee\beta+1)}e^{-(t-1)(\alpha+\beta+2)}\qquad\textrm{when}\quad\alpha\vee\beta\geq-1/2

and

\bigg|G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)-\frac{1}{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}\bigg|\leq\frac{27\,\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+2)}{5\cdot 2^{\alpha+\beta+2}\Gamma(\alpha+2)\Gamma(\beta+2)}e^{-(t-1)(\alpha+\beta+2)}\qquad\textrm{when}\quad\alpha\vee\beta<-1/2.

Applications

The Jacobi heat kernel is intimately related to a number of other heat kernels that are of interest and importance.
The list includes, above all, the spherical heat kernel and, more generally, heat kernels on all
compact rank one symmetric spaces. Further, it encloses heat kernels corresponding to Fourier–Bessel and Fourier–Dini expansions,
as well as heat kernels associated with multi-dimensional balls, simplices, cones, conical surfaces and other solids and surfaces
of revolution; see Section
6.4
for more details.

In this connection, our results for the Jacobi heat kernel can be used to deduce similar results for the other heat kernels just mentioned.
As pivotal examples, we obtain sharp bounds with explicit multiplicative constants for heat kernels,
viz. Brownian motion
4
4

4

The heat kernels we consider correspond to time-scaled Brownian motions
B_{2t}
.

transition probability densities, on all compact rank one symmetric spaces.
The results stated below focus on small times, since this is the real essence of the matter.
Fully explicit, sharp medium and large time bounds can then be derived in a straightforward manner,
as discussed in Section
6
.

Sharp global estimates for the spherical heat kernel were obtained only recently in
[
27
]
, and the result was new
even for the ordinary sphere of dimension
2
. We can now significantly enhance that result. Let
S^{d}
be the Euclidean
unit sphere of dimension
d\geq 2
, equipped with the standard, non-normalized surface area measure.
The corresponding heat kernel depends on its arguments only through their spherical geodesic distance,
say
\phi
, and thus it can be viewed as a one-dimensional function
[0,\pi]\ni\phi\mapsto K_{t}^{d}(\phi)
.
See the beginning of Section
3
and Section
6.1
.

We first distinguish the case of the ordinary
2
-dimensional sphere, see (
6.3
) in Section
6.1
,
since it seems to be fundamental from the perspective of real-world applications.

Theorem 4
.

Let
0<T\leq 2\pi^{2}
. Then

\frac{2\pi^{-1/2}e^{-\pi/4}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}t\vee(\pi-\phi)}}\,\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg)\leq K_{t}^{2}(\phi)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\pi^{3/2}e^{T/4}}{\sqrt{\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}t\vee(\pi-\phi)}}\,\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg)

for
\phi\in[0,\pi]
and
0<t\leq T
.

Next, we give explicit sharp bounds for heat kernels on spheres of higher dimensions, see Section
6.1
.

Theorem 5
.

Let
d\geq 3
and
\phi\in[0,\pi]
. Then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{d}(\phi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(11/10)\cdot(3/4)^{d-1}}{\big[\mathbb{D}_{d/2-1}t\,\vee\,\pi(\pi-\phi)/2\big]^{(d-1)/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),

\displaystyle K_{t}^{d}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(8/5)\cdot(24/5)^{d-1}}{\big[\mathbb{D}_{d/2-1}t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi\big]^{(d-1)/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg)

for
0<t\leq 2/(d-1/2)
, where
\mathbb{D}_{d/2-1}=\Gamma(d/2+1/2)^{2/(d-1)}
. Moreover,

K_{t}^{d}(\phi)\leq\frac{7\cdot(9/4)^{d-1}}{\big[\mathbb{D}_{d/2-1}t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi\big]^{(d-1)/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg)

for
0<t\leq 1/(d-1/2)^{2}
.

A simple scaling argument provides analogous results for spheres of arbitrary radii.
In the above statements, we omit the one-dimensional sphere, i.e. the torus, since that case is classical;
see Section
6.1
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a compact rank one symmetric space.
Such spaces are completely classified, they are Euclidean spheres, projective spaces over
reals, complex numbers and quaternions, and the projective plane over octonions; see Section
6.2
.
Each
\mathbb{M}
is a Riemannian manifold. Thus, we consider
\mathbb{M}
equipped with the Riemannian geodesic distance
and the Riemannian volume measure. For simplicity, we assume that the diameter of
\mathbb{M}
is
\pi
;
this does not affect generality, by a simple scaling.
The heat kernel corresponding to
\mathbb{M}
depends on its arguments only through their geodesic distance, say
\phi
,
and can therefore be interpreted as a one-dimensional function
[0,\pi]\ni\phi\mapsto K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)
.
See Section
6.2
.
Sharp estimates for
K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)
with unspecified multiplicative constants were established recently in
[
28
]
.

In the following statements, the lowest possible dimensions are excluded, since in those cases the spaces in question are identified
with spheres of certain dimensions. For the results presented in Theorems
6
–
9
,
see Section
6.2
.

Theorem 6
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be the real projective space of dimension
d\geq 2
and diameter
\pi
. Let
\phi\in[0,\pi]
.

(i)

Assume
d
is odd. Then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{5}\cdot\Big(\frac{17}{5}\Big)^{(d-1)/2}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{d},

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq 7\cdot\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)^{(d-1)/2}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{d^{2}}.

Moreover, for
d=3
and any
0<T\leq 2\pi^{2}

K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)\leq\pi e^{T/4}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq T.

(ii)

Assume
d
is even. If
d\geq 4
, then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16}{5}\cdot 23^{(d-1)/2}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{8}{d-1/2},

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq 14\cdot 5^{(d-1)/2}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{(d-1/2)^{2}}.

If
d=2
, then

K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)\leq 4\pi e^{T/16}\,\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq T,

for any
0<T\leq 8\pi^{2}
.

We remark that under the assumptions of Theorem
6
the lower bound

K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)\geq\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad t>0,

holds by a general theory, see Section
6.2
for the details.

Theorem 7
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be the complex projective space of real dimension
d\geq 4
and diameter
\pi
. Let
\phi\in[0,\pi]
. Then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3/5}{\sqrt{\pi t/4\,\vee\,\pi(\pi-\phi)/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad t>0,

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(16/5)\cdot(17/5)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\pi t/4\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{d+1},

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{14\cdot(\pi/2)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\pi t/4\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{(d+1)^{2}}.

Theorem 8
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be the quaternionic projective space of real dimension
d\geq 8
and diameter
\pi
. Let
\phi\in[0,\pi]
. Then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1/2}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{1}\,t\,\vee\,\pi(\pi-\phi)/2}\big]^{3/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad t>0,

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(23/5)\cdot(17/5)^{d/2+1}}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{1}\,t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}\big]^{3/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{d+3},

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{20\cdot(\pi/2)^{d/2+1}}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{1}\,t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}\big]^{3/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{(d+3)^{2}},

where
\mathbb{D}_{1}=(3\sqrt{\pi}/4)^{2/3}
.

Recall that the Cayley projective plane over octonions has real dimension
16
.

Theorem 9
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be the Cayley projective plane of diameter
\pi
. Let
\phi\in[0,\pi]
. Then

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1/4}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{3}\,t\,\vee\,\pi(\pi-\phi)/2}\big]^{7/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{8}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad t>0,

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{19\cdot(17/5)^{11}}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{3}\,t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}\big]^{7/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{8}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{23},

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\mathbb{M}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{80\cdot(\pi/2)^{11}}{\big[{\mathbb{D}_{3}\,t\,\vee\,2(\pi-\phi)/\pi}\big]^{7/2}}\,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{8}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\phi^{2}}{4t}\bigg),\qquad 0<t\leq\frac{4}{529},

where
\mathbb{D}_{3}=(105\sqrt{\pi}/16)^{2/7}
.

By the unified proof and arguments parallel to those leading to Theorems
4
–
9
,
see Sections
6.1
and
6.2
,
one can also prove sharp bounds with explicit multiplicative constants for the (negative) derivatives of the heat kernels on
all compact rank one symmetric spaces, viewed as
functions of geodesic distances. This is based on the differentiation rule from Lemma
2.1
.
Such bounds with unspecified multiplicative constants were obtained earlier in
[
27
, Cor. 2]
and
[
28
, Cor. 4.2]
.

We note that, excluding trivial cases,
sharp heat kernel estimates with explicit numerical values of multiplicative constants appear to be very rare in the literature.
In fact, we are not able to point out any reasonable concrete examples.

Structure of the paper

Section
2
consists of technical preliminaries. First, it provides a description of the general notation used in the paper,
and then it gathers various facts, formulas and relevant results related to the Jacobi setting, as well as other tools
and auxiliary results.
Section
3
contains estimates related to odd-dimensional spheres, which constitute the foundations
of the unified proof of the Jacobi heat kernel bounds.
Section
4
presents the core of the unified proof, which is divided into Steps A–F concluded respectively with
Lemmas
A
–
F
. It also contains some refinements, stated in Lemmas
G
and
H
,
for certain special cases that arise in applications.
Section
5
deals with medium and large time estimates.
Finally, in Section
6
, previous results are applied to find explicit multiplicative constants in the Jacobi
heat kernel bounds and in heat kernel estimates on compact rank one symmetric spaces.
Also, comments pertaining to other interrelated heat kernels are given.

A tabulation of constants and selected auxiliary functions used throughout the paper can be found in Appendix.

2.
Technical preliminaries

We shall use various tools from
[
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
.
We shall enhance some of them so that multiplicative constants they provide are explicit.
We will also need new tools and auxiliary results.
All that is gathered in this section.

2.1.
General notation

The set of natural numbers
\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
is denoted by
\mathbb{N}
.
By
\vee
and
\wedge
we indicate operations of taking maximum and minimum, respectively, of two quantities.
The priority of executing
\vee
and
\wedge
is always lower than multiplication/division but higher than addition/subtraction.

For non-negative quantities
X,Y
and positive quantities
c,C

X\simeq\left\{C\atop c\right\}Y\qquad\equiv\qquad cY\leq X\leq CY.

Multiplicative constants in lower bounds will always be denoted by lower case letters, and those in upper bounds by capital letters.
Positive numerical constants (independent of any parameters) will be denoted by Gothic letters with exception of
classical constants like
\pi
,
e
and
\gamma
.
Approximations of numerical expressions and constants are given (with a few exceptions) up to six decimal places after the dot,
with rounding to the closest rational number of this form.

In notation of the Jacobi heat kernel and related objects where both the parameters
\alpha,\beta
appear, we abbreviate “
\alpha,\!\alpha
”
(the Jacobi-ultraspherical case when the two parameters coincide) to “
\alpha
”,
e.g.
G_{t}^{\lambda,\lambda}(x,y)\equiv G_{t}^{\lambda}(x,y)
,
h_{n}^{\lambda,\lambda}\equiv h_{n}^{\lambda}
, etc.

2.2.
Facts and formulas related to the Jacobi heat kernel

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
.
The kernel
G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
is a strictly positive smooth function of
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times[-1,1]^{2}
.
For each
y\in[-1,1]
fixed, in the variables
(t,x)
it satisfies the heat equation based on the Jacobi operator
J^{\alpha,\beta}
,

(2.1)

\Big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}+J^{\alpha,\beta}_{x}\Big)G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=0,\qquad x\in[-1,1],\quad t>0.

Further, we have the following.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
28
, Lem. 3.3]
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}x}\,G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,1)=2(\alpha+1)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+2)}G_{t}^{\alpha+1,\beta+1}(x,1),\qquad x\in[-1,1],\quad t>0.

In particular, the function
x\mapsto G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,1)
is strictly increasing on
[-1,1]
.

Following
[
29
, Sec. 4]
, for
-3/2<\lambda\leq-1
we consider an auxiliary function

H_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=\frac{\Gamma(\lambda+3/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(\lambda+2)}+\frac{1}{2^{2\lambda+1}\Gamma(\lambda+2)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-t2n(2n+2\lambda+1)}\frac{(4n+2\lambda+1)\Gamma(2n+2\lambda+1)}{\Gamma(2n+\lambda+1)}P_{2n}^{\lambda}(x),

which is well-defined for
x\in[-1,1]
and
t>0
. This function can be regarded as an extension for
-3/2<\lambda\leq-1

of (a constant times) the even part in
x
of the Jacobi-ultraspherical kernel
G_{t}^{\lambda}(x,1)
.
Note that
H_{t}^{\lambda}(x)
is a smooth function of

(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times[-1,1]
which satisfies the heat equation based on the Jacobi-ultraspherical operator
J^{\lambda}
,

(2.2)

\Big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}+J_{x}^{\lambda}\Big)H_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=0,\qquad x\in[-1,1],\quad t>0.

Furthermore, we have the following.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
29
, Lem. 4.2]
)
.

Let
-3/2<\lambda\leq-1
. Then

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}x}\,H_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=2e^{-t(2\lambda+2)}\Big[G_{t}^{\lambda+1}(x,1)\Big]_{\emph{odd}},\qquad x\in[-1,1],\quad t>0,

where the subscript ‘odd’ indicates the odd part of the function in
x
. Similarly,

\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}x^{2}}\,H_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=4(\lambda+2)e^{-t(4\lambda+6)}\Big[G_{t}^{\lambda+2}(x,1)\Big]_{\emph{even}},\qquad x\in[-1,1],\quad t>0,

where the subscript ‘even’ indicates the even part of the function in
x
.

For
-1<\alpha\neq-1/2
denote

\Pi_{\alpha}(u)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}\int_{0}^{u}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w.

Observe that
\Pi_{\alpha}
is an odd function in
-1<u<1
, which is increasing if
\alpha>-1/2
and decreasing if
\alpha<-1/2
.
For
\alpha>-1/2
the measure
{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}
is a probability measure in the interval
[-1,1]
. The weak limit of
{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}

as
\alpha\searrow-1/2
is

{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}:=\frac{\delta_{-1}+\delta_{1}}{2},

where
\delta_{\pm 1}
is a unit point mass at
\pm 1
. For
-1<\alpha<-1/2
the distribution derivative
{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}

is a local measure in
(-1,1)
and its density is negative, even, and non-integrable in
(-1,1)
.

The next result is a
reduction formula
. It was obtained first under the restriction
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
in
[
25
]
,
and more recently in its full generality in
[
29
]
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
29
, Thm. 4.3]
)
.

Let
t>0
and
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
. Then the following identities hold, with all integrations taken over
[-1,1]^{2}
.

(i)

If
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
, then

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}{h_{0}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}}\,G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\iint G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v).

(ii)

If
-1<\beta<-1/2\leq\alpha
, then

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}{h_{0}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}}\,G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\iint\Big\{-2(\alpha+\beta+3/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,\Pi_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(v)\Big\}.

(iii)

If
-1<\alpha<-1/2\leq\beta
, then

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}{h_{0}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}}\,G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\iint\Big\{-2(\alpha+\beta+3/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)\Big\}.

(iv)

If
-1<\alpha,\beta<-1/2
and
\alpha+\beta>-3/2
, then

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}}{h_{0}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}}\,G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\iint\Big\{(\alpha+\beta+3/2)(\alpha+\beta+5/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+2)}\sin\theta\sin\varphi

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+5/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}u\,\Pi_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2(\alpha+\beta+3/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(v)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2(\alpha+\beta+3/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(u)\,\Pi_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(v)\Big\}.

(v)

If
-1<\alpha,\beta<-1/2
and
\alpha+\beta\leq-3/2
, then

\displaystyle\frac{h_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}\,\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+5/2)}G_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(\cos\theta,\cos\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad=\iint\Big\{(\alpha+\beta+5/2)e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+2)}\sin\theta\sin\varphi

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+5/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}u\,\Pi_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(v)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2e^{-t(\alpha+\beta+3/2)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+3/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},1\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(u)\,\Pi_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+H_{t/4}^{\alpha+\beta+1/2}\Big(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{-1/2}(v)\Big\}.

The result below is essentially the
comparison principle
obtained in
[
25
, Thm. 3.5]
.
Here we state a slightly stronger version, which is implicitly contained in the proof in
[
25
]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>-1
. Given
\epsilon,\delta\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq-\epsilon/2
,
\beta\geq-\delta/2
,

\big[(1-x)(1-y)\big]^{\epsilon/2}\big[(1+x)(1+y)\big]^{\delta/2}{G}_{t}^{\alpha+\epsilon,\beta+\delta}(x,y)\leq e^{\frac{\epsilon+\delta}{2}(\alpha+\beta+1+\frac{\epsilon+\delta}{2})t}{G}_{t}^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)

for all
x,y\in[-1,1]
and
t>0
, with the convention that
(1\pm x)^{0}=1
for
x=\mp 1
.
Moreover, the assumption
\alpha\geq-\epsilon/2
can be deleted if
\epsilon=0
,
and similarly for the assumption
\beta\geq-\delta/2
in case
\delta=0
.

2.3.
Estimates related to the density
\Pi_{\alpha}(u)

The following result is a refinement of
[
26
, Lem. 2.2]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\alpha\in(-1,-1/2)
be fixed. Then the density
|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|
defines a finite measure on
[-1,1]
and

|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|\simeq\left\{K_{\alpha}\atop k_{\alpha}\right\}\,|u|(1-|u|)^{\alpha+1/2},\qquad u\in(-1,1),

where

k_{\alpha}:=\frac{2^{\alpha-1/2}|\alpha+1/2|\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)},\qquad K_{\alpha}:=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)}.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha\in(-1,-1/2)
. Since the relevant expressions are even, we may restrict our attention to
u\in(0,1)
.
Observe that

\displaystyle|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,|\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)|}\int_{0}^{u}(1-w^{2})^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,|\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)|}\int_{0}^{u}(1-w)^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)}\big[(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}-1\big]

and similarly

|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|\geq\frac{2^{\alpha-1/2}\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)}\big[(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}-1\big].

Now let

f(u)=\frac{(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}-1}{u(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}},\qquad u\in(0,1).

Notice that
f
is increasing (since
f^{\prime}>0
, as can be verified by an elementary analysis)
and
f(0^{+})=-(\alpha+1/2)
,
f(1^{-})=1
. It follows that

|\alpha+1/2|u(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}\leq(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}-1\leq u(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}.

This together with the previous estimates allows one to conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\alpha\in(-1,-1/2)
be fixed. Then

\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|\,{\rm d}u\simeq\left\{K_{\alpha}\atop k_{\alpha}\right\}\,\frac{1}{\alpha+3/2}(1-u^{\prime})^{\alpha+3/2},\qquad u^{\prime}\in[0,1].

Proof.

Denote the integral in question by
I
. To show the upper bound we use Lemma
2.5
getting

I\leq K_{\alpha}\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}u(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}\,{\rm d}u\leq K_{\alpha}\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}(1-u)^{\alpha+1/2}\,{\rm d}u=\frac{K_{\alpha}}{\alpha+3/2}(1-u^{\prime})^{\alpha+3/2}.

For the lower bound, instead of Lemma
2.5
, we use the definition of
\Pi_{\alpha}(u)
and Fubini’s theorem to write

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,|\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)|}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\,I

\displaystyle=\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}\int_{0}^{u}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}u=\int_{0}^{1}(1-w^{2})^{\alpha-1/2}\int_{u^{\prime}\vee w}^{1}{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{u^{\prime}}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}w+\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\int_{w}^{1}{\rm d}u\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=(1-u^{\prime})\int_{0}^{u^{\prime}}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w+\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}\big(1-w^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}(1-w)\,{\rm d}w.

Neglecting the first term in the last sum and estimating the second one gives

\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,|\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)|}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\,I\geq 2^{\alpha-1/2}\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}(1-w)^{\alpha+1/2}\,{\rm d}w=\frac{2^{\alpha-1/2}}{\alpha+3/2}(1-u^{\prime})^{\alpha+3/2}.

The conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\alpha\in(-1,-1/2)
be fixed. Then

\int_{u^{\prime}}^{1}|\Pi_{\alpha}(u)|\,{\rm d}u\simeq\left\{L_{\alpha}\atop l_{\alpha}\right\}\,\frac{{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha+2}(u^{\prime})}{{\rm d}u^{\prime}},\qquad u^{\prime}\in[0,1],

where

l_{\alpha}:=\frac{|\alpha+1/2|}{4(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)},\qquad L_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)}.

Proof.

Let
u^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. We have

\frac{{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha+2}(u^{\prime})}{{\rm d}u^{\prime}}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+3)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+5/2)}\big[(1-u^{\prime})(1+u^{\prime})\big]^{\alpha+3/2},

hence

(1-u^{\prime})^{\alpha+3/2}\simeq\left\{1\atop 2^{-\alpha-3/2}\right\}\,\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+5/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha+3)}\frac{{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha+2}(u^{\prime})}{{\rm d}u^{\prime}}.

Combining this with Lemma
2.6
we get the desired bounds.
∎

Finally, for further reference we state a simple observation. Fix
\alpha>-1/2
. Then

\frac{{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)}{{\rm d}u}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}\big(1-u^{2}\big)^{\alpha-1/2}\simeq\left\{1\vee 2^{\alpha-1/2}\atop 1\wedge 2^{\alpha-1/2}\right\}\,\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}(1-u)^{\alpha-1/2}

for
u\in[0,1)
. Consequently,

(2.3)

(1-u)^{\alpha-1/2}\simeq\left\{N_{\alpha}\atop n_{\alpha}\right\}\,\frac{{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)}{{\rm d}u},\qquad u\in[0,1),

with

n_{\alpha}:=\big(1\wedge 2^{1/2-\alpha}\big)\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)},\qquad N_{\alpha}:=\big(1\vee 2^{1/2-\alpha}\big)\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}.

2.4.
Estimates related to the function
F(u,v)

Let
0\leq\theta,\varphi\leq\pi
. For
0\leq u,v\leq 1
denote

(2.4)

F(u,v)=F_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v):=\bigg[\arccos\bigg(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\bigg)\bigg]^{2}.

Then
0\leq F(1,1)=(\theta-\varphi)^{2}/4\leq F(u,v)\leq\pi^{2}/4=F(0,0)
.

The following result is a refinement of
[
29
, Lem. 5.4]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

The bounds

(2.5)

F(u,v)-F(1,1)\simeq\left\{\mathfrak{B}\atop\mathfrak{b}\right\}\big[\theta\varphi(1-u)+(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)(1-v)\big]

hold for
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
u,v\in[0,1]
, with
\mathfrak{b}:=2/\pi^{2}
and
\mathfrak{B}:=1/2
.
The constants
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are optimal.

Proof.

We first show the lower bound. By continuity we may assume that
\theta\neq\varphi
. Then, in particular,
F(u,v)>0
.
Notice that by differentiating in
u
and
v
the equation

\cos\sqrt{F(u,v)}=u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}

we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{u}F(u,v)

\displaystyle=-2\frac{\sqrt{F(u,v)}}{\sin\sqrt{F(u,v)}}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2},

\displaystyle\partial_{v}F(u,v)

\displaystyle=-2\frac{\sqrt{F(u,v)}}{\sin\sqrt{F(u,v)}}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}.

Fix
u,v\in[0,1]
. By the Mean Value Theorem there exists
c=(c_{1},c_{2})\in[0,1]^{2}
depending on
u
and
v
such that

\displaystyle F(u,v)-F(1,1)

\displaystyle=\partial_{u}F(c)(u-1)+\partial_{v}F(c)(v-1)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\sqrt{F(c)}}{\sin\sqrt{F(c)}}\Big[\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}(1-u)+\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}(1-v)\Big].

Since we have
\sqrt{F(c)}\in(0,\pi/2]
, in view of the simple estimates

1\leq\frac{x}{\sin x}\leq\frac{\pi}{2},\qquad\frac{2x}{\pi}\leq\sin x\leq x,\qquad\frac{\pi-2x}{\pi}\leq\cos x\leq\frac{\pi-2x}{2}\qquad x\in(0,\pi/2],

we obtain the lower bound in (
2.5
).
5
5

5

Proceeding in a similar way we can also get the upper bound, but with non-optimal constant
\pi/4
.

Next, we justify the upper bound. Observe that this is equivalent to showing that
G(u,v)\geq 0
,
u,v\in[0,1]^{2}
, where

\displaystyle G(u,v)=G_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v):=\frac{1}{2}\big[(1-u)\theta\varphi+(1-v)(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)\big]+(\theta-\varphi)^{2}/4-F_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v).

Elementary computations lead to

\displaystyle\partial_{u}^{2}G(u,v)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{u}^{2}F(u,v)=\frac{-2\big(\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}\big)^{2}}{1-(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[1-\arccos\bigg(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\bigg)\frac{u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}}{\sqrt{1-(u\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}+v\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2})^{2}}}\right].

Since we have
x\arccos x\leq\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
,
x\in(0,1)
, which can be easily checked because the function

(0,1)\ni x\mapsto\arccos x-{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}/{x}
is increasing, we see that
\partial_{u}^{2}G(u,v)\leq 0
. Consequently,
for any fixed
v\in[0,1]
the function

[0,1]\ni u\mapsto G(u,v)
is concave and its minimal value is attained at one of the endpoints.
By the symmetry
G_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v)=G_{\pi-\theta,\pi-\varphi}(v,u)
we obtain

G(u,v)\geq\inf_{\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]}G_{\theta,\varphi}(0,0)\wedge G_{\theta,\varphi}(0,1)\wedge G_{\theta,\varphi}(1,1)
.
Since
G(1,1)=0
and
G(0,0)=(\theta+\varphi-\pi)^{2}/4
, it suffices to check that
G(0,1)\geq 0
.

Notice that showing
G(0,1)\geq 0
is equivalent to justifying that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\theta^{2}+\varphi^{2}}}{2}-\arccos\bigg(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\bigg)\geq 0,\qquad\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi].

Let

\displaystyle X(\theta)=X_{\varphi}(\theta):=\frac{\sqrt{\theta^{2}+\varphi^{2}}}{2}-\arccos\bigg(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}\bigg).

Since
X(0)=0
it suffices to prove that
X^{\prime}(\theta)\geq 0
. Observe that

\displaystyle X^{\prime}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\theta}{\sqrt{\theta^{2}+\varphi^{2}}}-\frac{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2}}{\sqrt{1-(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2})^{2}}}\right].

Since
1-(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2})^{2}=\sin^{2}\frac{\varphi}{2}+(\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2})^{2}
,
it is not hard to see that
X^{\prime}(\theta)\geq 0
is equivalent to showing that

\displaystyle\theta\sin\frac{\varphi}{2}\geq\varphi\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\frac{\varphi}{2},\qquad\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi].

The latter inequality, however, is easily seen to hold true.

Finally, let us look at the optimality of
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{b}
.
Letting
u=v
,
\theta=\varphi=0
and then
u\to 1^{-}
we see that
\mathfrak{b}
is optimal.
On the other hand, taking
u=v=0
,
\varphi=0
and then
\theta\to\pi^{-}
we get the optimality of
\mathfrak{B}
.

The proof of Lemma
2.8
is finished.
∎

2.5.
Estimates of various integrals

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\omega\in[1/2,2]
. Then

\int_{\xi}^{\infty}u^{\omega-1}e^{-u}\,{\rm d}u\simeq\left\{M_{\omega}\atop m_{\omega}\right\}\,(1+\xi)^{\omega-1}e^{-\xi},\qquad\xi\geq 0,

with the constants

m_{\omega}:=\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{e\,\omega},\qquad M_{\omega}:=\frac{2}{(1-1/e)^{2}\,\omega}.

To justify the above we need the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\omega\in[1/2,2]
. Then

\int_{a}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\simeq\left\{2/\omega\atop(1-2^{-\omega})/\omega\right\}\,b^{\omega-1}(b-a),\qquad 0\leq a<b<\infty.

Proof.

Case 1:
b\geq 2a
.
Here we have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\leq\int_{0}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u=b^{\omega}/\omega\leq\frac{2}{\omega}(b-a)b^{\omega-1}.

On the other hand,

\int_{a}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\geq\int_{b/2}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u=\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}b^{\omega}\geq\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}(b-a)b^{\omega-1}.

Case 2:
a\leq b<2a
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\leq(b-a)\begin{cases}b^{\omega-1}&\text{ if }\;\;\omega\geq 1\\
(b/2)^{\omega-1}&\text{ if }\;\;\omega<1\end{cases},

and

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\geq(b-a)\begin{cases}(b/2)^{\omega-1}&\text{ if }\;\;\omega\geq 1\\
b^{\omega-1}&\text{ if }\;\;\omega<1\end{cases}.

This implies Lemma
2.10
, since for
\omega\in[1,2]
we have

\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}\wedge 2^{1-\omega}=\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}
and
\frac{2}{\omega}\vee 1=\frac{2}{\omega}
,
and for
\omega\in[1/2,1]
we have
\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}\wedge 1=\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{\omega}
and

\frac{2}{\omega}\vee 2^{1-\omega}=\frac{2}{\omega}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

Using Lemma
2.10
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\xi}^{\infty}u^{\omega-1}e^{-u}\,{\rm d}u\geq\int_{\xi}^{\xi+1}u^{\omega-1}e^{-u}\,{\rm d}u\geq e^{-\xi-1}\int_{\xi}^{\xi+1}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\geq\frac{1-2^{-\omega}}{e\,\omega}(\xi+1)^{\omega-1}e^{-\xi}.

This is the lower bound in question.

Next, we prove the upper bound. Using again Lemma
2.10
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\xi}^{\infty}u^{\omega-1}e^{-u}\,{\rm d}u\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}e^{-\xi-k}\int_{\xi+k}^{\xi+k+1}u^{\omega-1}\,{\rm d}u\leq\frac{2}{\omega}e^{-\xi}\sum_{k\geq 0}e^{-k}(\xi+k+1)^{\omega-1}.

Further, observe that

\displaystyle(\xi+k+1)^{\omega-1}\leq(\xi+1)^{\omega-1}(1+k).

Combining this with the identity
\sum_{k\geq 0}(k+1)e^{-k}=(1-1/e)^{-2}
we get the desired bound.
∎

Denote

\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}:=\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)^{1/{(\alpha+1/2)}},\qquad\alpha>-3/2,

with the value for
\alpha=-1/2
understood in the limiting sense,

\mathbb{D}_{-1/2}=\lim_{\alpha\to-1/2}\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}=e^{-\gamma},

where
\gamma=-\Gamma^{\prime}(1)\approx 0.577216
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.
Note that
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
is strictly increasing in
\alpha
.
6
6

6

For the monotonicity of
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
on
(-1/2,\infty)
, see e.g.
[
33
, Sec. 1]
.
To show that
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
is strictly increasing on
(-3/2,-1/2)
it is enough to verify that

(\mathbb{D}_{\alpha})^{\prime}=(\alpha+1/2)^{-2}\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}[(\alpha+1/2)\psi(\alpha+3/2)-\log\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)]>0
for
\alpha\in(-3/2,-1/2)
;
here
\psi
is the logarithmic derivative of
\Gamma
(the digamma function). Let
H_{\alpha}
be the expression in the last square brackets.
Since
H_{-1/2}=0
, the task boils down to checking that
(H_{\alpha})^{\prime}=(\alpha+1/2)\psi^{\prime}(\alpha+3/2)<0
for
\alpha\in(-3/2,-1/2)
.
This, however, follows instantly from known positivity of the trigamma function
\psi^{\prime}
, see e.g.
[
30
, Chap. 5,
\lx@sectionsign
5.15, Form. 5.15.1]
.

Further, one has
\mathbb{D}_{-1}=1/\pi
,
\mathbb{D}_{0}=\pi/4
and
\mathbb{D}_{1/2}=1
.

The next result is a refinement of
[
29
, Lem. 5.5]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\alpha\geq-1/2
. Then

\int_{[0,1]}\exp\Big(-\xi(1-s)\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(s)\simeq\left\{B_{\alpha}\atop b_{\alpha}\right\}\,\big(\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\vee\xi\big)^{-\alpha-1/2},\qquad\xi\geq 0,

with the constants

b_{\alpha}:=\big(1\wedge 2^{\alpha-1/2}\big)\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\big(-\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\big),\qquad B_{\alpha}:=\big(1\vee 2^{\alpha-1/2}\big)\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}},\qquad\alpha>-1/2,

and
B_{-1/2}:=1/2=:b_{-1/2}
.

Remark 2.12
.

It is known (see
[
33
, Sec. 1]
and references given there) that the function
x\mapsto[\Gamma(x+1)]^{1/x}/(x+1)
is
strictly decreasing for
x>0
, and it has limits
e^{-\gamma}
as
x\to 0^{+}
and
1/e
as
x\to\infty
.
Thus we have the bounds

(2.6)

\frac{\alpha+3/2}{e}<\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}<\frac{\alpha+3/2}{e^{\gamma}},\qquad\alpha>-1/2,

which show that
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
depends roughly linearly on the parameter.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}=c_{1}(\alpha+c_{2})
. The condition
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}/\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\to 1
as
\alpha\to\infty
is equivalent to

c_{1}=1/e
. Then the choice of
c_{2}
can be made e.g. by imposing the condition
\mathbb{D}_{-1/2}=\mathbb{E}_{-1/2}
, which
implies
c_{2}=e^{1-\gamma}+1/2\approx 2.026205
. In this case
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}=(\alpha+1/2)/e+e^{-\gamma}
and from (
2.6
)
it follows that

\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\simeq\left\{e^{1-\gamma}\atop e^{\gamma-1}\right\}\,\mathbb{E}_{\alpha},\qquad\alpha\geq-1/2.

Note that
e^{\gamma-1}\approx 0.655220
and
e^{1-\gamma}\approx 1.526205
. It can be shown that in fact

\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\simeq\left\{11/10\atop 1\right\}\,\mathbb{E}_{\alpha},\qquad\alpha\geq-1/2,

but we will not provide the proof;
anyhow, these bounds become evident just by plotting the ratio
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}/\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}

with the aid of any common software tool for mathematics.

Proof of Lemma
2.11
.

We may assume that
\alpha>-1/2
, since for
\alpha=-1/2
the integral is equal to
1/2
(in particular, it is independent of
\xi
)
and the lemma clearly holds. Then, in view of the definition of
{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}
and by a simple change of variable, we have

(2.7)

\int_{[0,1]}\exp\Big(-\xi(1-s)\Big)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(s)\simeq\left\{1\vee 2^{\alpha-1/2}\atop 1\wedge 2^{\alpha-1/2}\right\}\,\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-u\xi}u^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}u.

Denote the last integral by
I
and let
\mathbb{D}>0
be a constant that will be specified later.

For
0\leq\xi\leq\mathbb{D}
we can estimate
I
either by neglecting the exponential factor or by replacing it by
its minimal value, getting

(2.8)

\frac{1}{\alpha+1/2}e^{-\mathbb{D}}\leq I\leq\frac{1}{\alpha+1/2},\qquad\xi\leq\mathbb{D}.

Let now
\xi\geq\mathbb{D}
. To get an upper bound we first change the variable and then enlarge the interval of integration
to
(0,\infty)
, obtaining

I=\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\int_{0}^{\xi}e^{-v}v^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}v\leq\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\,\Gamma(\alpha+1/2).

For a lower bound we write

I\geq\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\int_{0}^{\mathbb{D}}e^{-v}v^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}v=\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\,\mathbb{D}^{\alpha+1/2}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\mathbb{D}w}w^{\alpha-1/2}\,{\rm d}w\geq\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\,\mathbb{D}^{\alpha+1/2}e^{-\mathbb{D}}\frac{1}{\alpha+1/2}.

Thus

(2.9)

\frac{1}{\alpha+1/2}\mathbb{D}^{\alpha+1/2}e^{-\mathbb{D}}\xi^{-\alpha-1/2}\leq I\leq\frac{1}{\alpha+1/2}\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)\xi^{-\alpha-1/2},\qquad\xi\geq\mathbb{D}.

Taking into account the structure of (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), we see that a natural choice for
\mathbb{D}
is
such that
\mathbb{D}^{\alpha+1/2}=\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)
, that is
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
. Then a combination of
(
2.8
) and (
2.9
) leads to

\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)e^{-\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}}\big(\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\vee\xi\big)^{-\alpha-1/2}\leq I\leq\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)\big(\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\vee\xi\big)^{-\alpha-1/2},\qquad\xi\geq 0.

This together with (
2.7
) proves the lemma.
∎

Bring in notation

(2.10)

\Psi_{\alpha}^{\kappa}(t,\theta,\varphi):=\big[\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}t\vee\kappa\theta\varphi\big]^{-\alpha-1/2}.

Note that for
\alpha>-1
and
\kappa>0
one has
(vide Theorem
1
and the expression
Z^{\alpha,\beta}(t;\theta,\varphi)
)

\Psi_{\alpha}^{\kappa}(t,\theta,\varphi)\simeq\left\{\big[{1}/({\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\vee\kappa})\big]^{\alpha+1/2}\vee\big[{2}/({\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\wedge\kappa})\big]^{\alpha+1/2}\atop\big[{1}/({\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\vee\kappa})\big]^{\alpha+1/2}\wedge\big[{2}/({\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\wedge\kappa})\big]^{\alpha+1/2}\right\}\;\frac{1}{(t+\theta\varphi)^{\alpha+1/2}},\qquad\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi],\quad t>0.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
. Then

\iint_{[0,1]^{2}}e^{-{F_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v)}/t}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)\simeq\left\{B_{\alpha}B_{\beta}\Psi_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{b}}(t,\theta,\varphi)\Psi_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{b}}(t,\pi-\theta,\pi-\varphi)\atop b_{\alpha}b_{\beta}\Psi_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{B}}(t,\theta,\varphi)\Psi_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{B}}(t,\pi-\theta,\pi-\varphi)\right\}\,t^{\alpha+\beta+1}e^{-{(\theta-\varphi)^{2}}/{(4t)}}

for
\theta,\varphi\in[0,\pi]
and
t>0
.

Proof.

We prove the upper bound, proving the lower one is analogous. Observe that

\iint_{[0,1]^{2}}e^{-{F_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v)}/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)=e^{-{(\theta-\varphi)^{2}}/{(4t)}}\iint_{[0,1]^{2}}e^{-{[F_{\theta,\varphi}(u,v)-F_{\theta,\varphi}(1,1)]}/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v).

It is enough to bound suitably the last double integral which we denote by
I
.

Using Lemma
2.8
and then Lemma
2.11
we get

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,1]}e^{-{\mathfrak{b}\theta\varphi}(1-u)/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,\int_{[0,1]}e^{-{\mathfrak{b}(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)}(1-v)/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)

\displaystyle\leq t^{\alpha+\beta+1}\frac{B_{\alpha}}{[\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}t\vee\mathfrak{b}\theta\varphi]^{\alpha+1/2}}\frac{B_{\beta}}{[\mathbb{D}_{\beta}t\vee\mathfrak{b}(\pi-\theta)(\pi-\varphi)]^{\beta+1/2}},

as desired.
∎

Assuming that
\alpha,\beta\geq-1/2
, for the case
\varphi=0
observe that

\iint_{[0,1]^{2}}e^{-{F_{\theta,0}(u,v)}/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\alpha}(u)\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,1]}e^{-{F_{\theta,0}(1,v)}/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v).

Then we have the following.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\beta\geq-1/2
. Then

\int_{[0,1]}e^{-{F_{\theta,0}(1,v)}/{t}}\,{\rm d}\Pi_{\beta}(v)\simeq\left\{B_{\beta}\Psi_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{b}}(t,\pi-\theta,\pi)\atop b_{\beta}\Psi_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{B}}(t,\pi-\theta,\pi)\right\}\,t^{\beta+1/2}e^{-{\theta^{2}}/{(4t)}},\qquad\theta\in[0,\pi],\quad t>0.

Proof.

The reasoning is the same as in the proof of Lemma
2.13
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

2.6.
Facts and estimates related to the gamma function

The duplication formula, cf. e.g.
[
30
, Chap. 5,
\lx@sectionsign
5.5, Form. 5.5.5]
,

\Gamma(2x)=\frac{2^{2x-1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(x)\Gamma(x+1/2),\qquad x\notin-\mathbb{N}/2,

is used implicitly across the paper without further mention.

In the sequel we will use the following bounds and a fact:

(2.11)

\sqrt{2\pi}x^{x-1/2}e^{-x}<\Gamma(x)<\sqrt{2\pi}x^{x-1/2}e^{-x}e^{1/(12x)},\qquad x>0,

(2.12)

\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(y)}<x^{x-y},\qquad 0<y<x,

(2.13)

\binom{x+y}{y}=\frac{\Gamma(x+y+1)}{\Gamma(x+1)\Gamma(y+1)}\quad\textrm{is strictly increasing in $y>0$ for each $x>0$ fixed.}

For inequalities (
2.11
), see e.g.
[
30
, Chap. 5,
\lx@sectionsign
5.6, Form. 5.6.1]
.
Estimate (
2.12
) can be concluded e.g. from Wendel’s inequality, see
[
41
, Form. (4)]
and is a consequence
of log-convexity of
\Gamma
. The fact (
2.13
) can be verified by observing that
\binom{x+y}{y}=\frac{1}{xB(x,y+1)}

and
z\mapsto B(x,z)
is strictly decreasing for
z>0
; here
B
is the beta function.

We will also need the following.

Lemma 2.15
.

For every integer
j\geq 1
,

\Gamma(j+1/2)>\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{e^{1/12}}\,j^{j}e^{-j}.

Proof.

Observe that

\Gamma(j+1/2)=(j-1/2)\cdot\ldots\cdot(1/2)\,\Gamma(1/2)=\frac{(2j-1)!!}{2^{j}}\sqrt{\pi}=\frac{(2j-1)!!}{2^{j}}\frac{(2j)!!}{2^{j}j!}\sqrt{\pi}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4^{j}}\frac{(2j)!}{j!}.

We shall use Stirling-type inequalities. It is known, see
[
34
]
, that for any integer
n\geq 1

n!=\sqrt{2\pi}\,n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}e^{r_{n}},

where
r_{n}
satisfies
1/(12n+1)<r_{n}<1/(12n)
. In particular, we have

(2.14)

\sqrt{2\pi}\,n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}<n!<\sqrt{2\pi}\,n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}e^{1/12},\qquad n\geq 1.

Thus,

\frac{(2j)!}{j!}\geq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{e^{1/12}}4^{j}j^{j}e^{-j},\qquad j\geq 1.

This together with the initial observation concludes the proof.
∎

2.7.
Estimates related to the Bessel function
K_{\nu}

We denote by
K_{\nu}
the modified Bessel function of the second kind (Macdonald function) of order
\nu
, see e.g.
[
40
]

for the definition and basic properties. For
\nu>-1/2
and positive arguments
K_{\nu}
can be represented as
(cf.
[
30
, Chap. 10,
\lx@sectionsign
10.32(i), Form. 10.32.8]
)

K_{\nu}(y)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}y^{\nu}}{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1/2)}\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-yu}\big(u^{2}-1\big)^{\nu-1/2}\,{\rm d}u,\qquad y>0.

The following result is a part of
[
31
, Thm. 2]
.
7
7

7

There seem to be no better upper bounds uniform in
x
and
\nu
for
K_{\nu}(x)
available up to date.

Lemma 2.16

(
[
31
]
)
.

Let
\beta>1/2
,
\nu\geq 0
and
y>0
. Then

\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{y^{2}}{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bigg)^{1/4}\bigg(\frac{\nu+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}{y}\bigg)^{\nu}\exp\bigg(-\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}+y+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)

provided that
\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}\geq 2\beta/(2\beta-1)
.

We now extract from the above a slightly less precise and less general bound, but of much simpler form that will be
convenient for our purpose.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\nu\geq 5/2
and
0<\epsilon\leq 2/3
. Then

\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)\leq\frac{2}{\pi}e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)}(1+\epsilon)^{\nu},\qquad y\geq\frac{2\nu}{3\epsilon}.

Proof.

We use Lemma
2.16
with
\beta=1
. Notice that in view of the assumption
\nu\geq 5/2
the condition

\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}\geq 2\beta/(2\beta-1)
is satisfied for all
y>0
. We have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{y^{2}}{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}\bigg)^{1/4}\bigg(\frac{\nu+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}{y}\bigg)^{\nu}\exp\bigg(-\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}+y+\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle<\frac{2}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\nu+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}{y}\bigg)^{\nu}\exp\bigg(-\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}+y+\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg).

Observe that the assumptions
0<\epsilon\leq 2/3
and
y\geq 2\nu/(3\epsilon)
imply
y\geq\nu
, so

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(-\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}+y+\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\frac{-\nu^{2}}{y+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{-\nu^{2}}{y+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\nu}\bigg).

It follows that for
\nu\geq 5/2
and
y\geq 2\nu/(3\epsilon)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)

\displaystyle<\frac{2}{\pi}e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)}\bigg[\frac{\nu+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}{y}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\nu}{y+\sqrt{y^{2}+\nu^{2}}}\bigg)\bigg]^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)}\bigg[\Big({\nu}/{y}+\sqrt{1+(\nu/y)^{2}}\Big)\exp\bigg(\frac{-\nu/y}{1+\sqrt{1+(\nu/y)^{2}}}\bigg)\bigg]^{\nu}.

It is elementary to check that

\Big(x+\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\Big)\exp\bigg(-\frac{x}{1+\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\bigg)\leq 1+\frac{2}{3}x,\qquad x\in[0,1].

Consequently,

\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)<\frac{2}{\pi}e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)}\bigg(1+\frac{2\nu}{3y}\bigg)^{\nu}\leq\frac{2}{\pi}e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)}(1+\epsilon)^{\nu},

which is the desired bound.
∎

Corollary 2.18
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{N}+1/2
and
0<\epsilon\leq 2/3
. Then

\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\pi}}e^{y}K_{\nu}(y)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{\nu-1/2},\qquad y\geq\frac{2\nu}{3\epsilon}.

Proof.

For
\nu\geq 9/2
this follows from Lemma
2.17
, since then

(1+\epsilon)^{-1/2}\geq(1+2/3)^{-1/2}>(2/\pi)e^{1/(9\sqrt{2}/2)}\geq(2/\pi)e^{1/(\sqrt{2}\nu)},\qquad\epsilon\in(0,2/3].

On the other hand, for
\nu=1/2,3/2,5/2,7/2
we can verify the bound directly using explicit expressions for
K_{\nu}
,
cf.
[
40
, Chap. III, Sec. 3
\cdot
71, Form. (12)]
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\sqrt{{2y}/{\pi}}\,e^{y}K_{1/2}(y)=1,&\qquad\sqrt{{2y}/{\pi}}\,e^{y}K_{3/2}(y)=1+1/y,\\
\sqrt{{2y}/{\pi}}\,e^{y}K_{5/2}(y)=1+{3}/y+{3}/{y^{2}},&\qquad\sqrt{{2y}/{\pi}}\,e^{y}K_{7/2}(y)=1+{6}/y+{15}/{y^{2}}+{15}/{y^{3}}.\end{array}

The relevant facts here are that
1/y\leq 3\epsilon/(2\nu)
and that
\epsilon^{n}
is decreasing in
n
.
We leave elementary details to the reader.
∎

3.
Estimates related to odd-dimensional spheres

Let
S^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
be the Euclidean unit sphere of dimension
d\geq 1
equipped with the standard
non-normalized area measure
\sigma_{d}
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{d}(\xi,\eta)
be the heat kernel on
(S^{d},\sigma_{d})
.
By rotational invariance,
\mathcal{K}_{t}^{d}(\xi,\eta)
depends on
\xi
and
\eta
only through their geodesic distance

\dist(\xi,\eta)=\arccos\langle\xi,\eta\rangle
on
S^{d}
. Thus it can be written in terms of a one-dimensional
function
K_{t}^{d}
on
[0,\pi]
,

\mathcal{K}_{t}^{d}(\xi,\eta)=K_{t}^{d}\big(\dist(\xi,\eta)\big),\qquad\xi,\eta\in S^{d},\quad t>0.

Denote by
W_{t}
the one-dimensional Gauss-Weierstrass kernel, and observe that
\vartheta_{t}
is its
2\pi
-periodization,

W_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}\exp\bigg({-\frac{x^{2}}{4t}}\bigg),\qquad\vartheta_{t}(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{t}(x+2\pi n).

It is known, see e.g.
[
27
, Sec. 2]
, that

(3.1)

K_{t}^{2N+1}(\varphi)=\frac{e^{tN^{2}}}{(2\pi)^{N}}(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi),\qquad N\geq 0,

where

(3.2)

D=D_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\sin\varphi}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\varphi}.

On the other hand, we have the connection (see
[
27
, Form. (4)]
)

(3.3)

K_{t}^{d}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{d-1}(S^{d-1})}G_{t}^{d/2-1}(\cos\varphi,1)=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{2\pi^{d/2}}G_{t}^{d/2-1}(\cos\varphi,1),\qquad d\geq 1;

here
\sigma_{0}(S^{0})=2
in case
d=1
. Thus

(3.4)

G_{t}^{N-1/2}(\cos\varphi,1)=\frac{\sqrt{4\pi}}{2^{N}\Gamma(N+1/2)}e^{tN^{2}}(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi),\qquad N\geq 0.

The aim of Section
3
is to estimate
(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)
in the restricted range
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]

and for
t>0
reasonably small. In view of (
3.4
), this will instantly imply analogous bounds for
G_{t}^{\lambda}(x,1)

when
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}-1/2
and
x\in[0,1]
.

3.1.
Lower bound for
(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)

To get a lower bound for
(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)
we shall appeal to a general theory.
Since
S^{d}
is a complete Riemannian manifold with positive Ricci curvature,
[
9
, Thm. 5.6.1]

8
8

8

The result was originally proved by Cheeger and Yau
[
4
]
, following an earlier work of Debiard, Gaveau
and Mazet
[
10
]
; they, in fact, gave a more general version applicable to manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded below.

implies

K_{t}^{d}(\varphi)\geq\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}\exp\bigg({-\frac{\varphi^{2}}{4t}}\bigg),\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi],\quad t>0.

Note that this estimate is sharp in the sense that (see the proof of
[
9
, Thm. 5.6.1]
)

(3.5)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}(4\pi t)^{d/2}K_{t}^{d}(0)=1.

Consequently, by (
3.1
), we get the following result.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
N\geq 0
. Then

(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)\geq\frac{1}{2^{N}}e^{-tN^{2}}t^{-N}W_{t}(\varphi),\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi],\quad t>0.

The bound of Proposition
3.1
is quite reasonable for, say,
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]
, and the multiplicative
constant is sharp because of (
3.5
). On the other hand, this bound is far from optimal for
\varphi
close to
\pi
.
We note that the methods developed below to obtain upper bounds for
(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)
can be adapted to
provide also the lower bound, but in general with worse multiplicative constant and smaller range of
t
.

3.2.
Upper bound for
(-D)^{N}\vartheta_{t}(\varphi)
and
t\lesssim 1/N

We consider
N\geq 1
and roughly follow a strategy from
[
27
]
, but with simplifications
(due to the focus only on the upper bound and the restricted range of
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]
) and significant refinements.

Let
F
be an even entire function (e.g. the Gaussian or the hyperbolic cosine).
It is elementary to show, see
[
27
, Lem. 3.1]
, that

(3.6)

D^{N}_{\varphi}\big[F(v\varphi)\big]=\sum_{j=1}^{N}v^{2j}L^{j}F(v\varphi)\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi),\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi/2],\quad v>0,\quad N\geq 1,

where

L=\frac{1}{z}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}z}

and
\Phi_{N,j}
are bounded functions on
[0,\pi/2]
. In (
3.6
) and other places below
D^{N}_{\varphi}[F(v\varphi)]

means that the operator
D^{N}
is applied to the function
\varphi\mapsto F(v\varphi)
, and
L^{j}F(v\varphi)

stands for
L^{j}F
evaluated at
v\varphi
.

To make (
3.6
) useful for our purpose, we need to estimate
\Phi_{N,j}
. To do that, we now make these factors more explicit.
Denote

(3.7)

\Psi(\varphi):=\frac{\varphi}{\sin\varphi}

so that
\Phi_{1,1}=\Psi
. As in the proof of
[
27
, Lem. 3.1]
, applying
D_{\varphi}
to term number
j
in (
3.6
)
and using the Leibniz rule to evaluate
D_{\varphi}[L^{j}F(v\varphi)]
we get

D_{\varphi}\big[v^{2j}L^{j}F(v\varphi)\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)\big]=v^{2j+2}L^{j+1}F(v\varphi)\frac{\varphi}{\sin\varphi}\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)+v^{2j}L^{j}F(v\varphi)\frac{1}{\sin\varphi}\Phi^{\prime}_{N,j}(\varphi).

From this we infer that

(3.8)

\Phi_{N,j}=\Psi\big[\Phi_{N-1,j-1}+L\Phi_{N-1,j}\big],\qquad N\geq 2,\quad 1\leq j\leq N,

with the convention that
\Phi_{N,j}=0
whenever
j\notin\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Observe that
\Phi_{N,N}=\Psi^{N}
.

The recurrence (
3.8
) is the same as that satisfied by Comtet’s
A
-polynomials investigated in
[
5
]
.
To be precise, Comtet used the ordinary differentiation operator instead of
L
, but this is irrelevant for the present
considerations since both the operators obey the Leibniz rule. Thus
\Phi_{N,j}
is identified with the polynomial

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}
in
[
5
]
. More precisely,
\Phi_{N,j}
is of the form

\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)=\mathbf{A}_{N,j}\big(\Psi(\varphi),L\Psi(\varphi),\ldots,L^{N-j}\Psi(\varphi)\big),

where

(3.9)

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{N-j})=\sum_{k_{0}+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{N-j}=N\atop k_{1}+2k_{2}+\ldots+(N-j)k_{N-j}=N-j}a_{N,j}(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})\;\lambda_{0}^{k_{0}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{N-j}^{k_{N-j}}.

Here the summation
9
9

9

The number of terms in (
3.9
) is
p(N-j)
, the number of integer partitions of
N-j
, see e.g.
[
6
, Chap. II]

and
[
36
, A000041]
.
No explicit formula for the partition function
p
is known. A classical result by Hardy and Littlewood is the asymptotic

p(n)\sim\frac{1}{4\sqrt{3}n}\exp\big(\pi\sqrt{\frac{2n}{3}}\big)
. Concerning estimates of
p(n)
, see for instance

[
22
]
and references given there.

is over all integer
k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j}\geq 0
satisfying the indicated conditions.
All the coefficients
a_{N,j}
appearing in (
3.9
) are strictly positive natural numbers.
Each
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}
is a homogeneous polynomial
10
10

10

Tabulation of the polynomials
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}
up to degree
7
is given in
[
5
, Sec. 5]
.
The sequence of coefficients
a_{N,j}(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})
ordered according to
N
,
j
and
k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j}

can be found in
[
36
, A139605]
(weights for expansions of iterated derivatives).

of degree
N
and weight
N-j
, namely

(3.10)

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\ldots,ab^{i}\lambda_{i},\ldots)=a^{N}b^{N-j}\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\ldots,\lambda_{i},\ldots).

We now establish a recurrence relation for the coefficients
a_{N,j}(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})
, which may be of independent
interest.
11
11

11

No closed formula for
a_{N,j}
seems to be available in the literature.
Nevertheless, some special cases can be computed explicitly. For instance, except
for the immediate case
\Phi_{N,N}=\Psi^{N}
, one has
\Phi_{N,N-1}={N\choose 2}\Psi^{N-1}L\Psi
for
N\geq 2
,

\Phi_{N,N-2}={N\choose 3}\Psi^{N-1}L^{2}\Psi+\frac{3N-5}{4}{N\choose 3}\Psi^{N-2}\big(L\Psi)^{2}
for
N\geq 3
,

\Phi^{N,N-3}={N\choose 4}\Psi^{N-1}L^{3}\Psi+2(N-2){N\choose 4}\Psi^{N-2}(L\Psi)(L^{2}\Psi)+\frac{(N-3)(N-2)}{2}{N\choose 4}\Psi^{N-3}(L\Psi)^{3}
for
N\geq 4
, furthermore

\Phi_{N,N-4}={N\choose 5}\Psi^{N-1}L^{4}\Psi+\frac{5N-13}{2}{N\choose 5}\Psi^{N-2}(L\Psi)(L^{3}\Psi)+\frac{15N^{2}-85N+116}{6}{N\choose 5}\Psi^{N-3}(L\Psi)^{2}(L^{2}\Psi)+\frac{15N^{3}-150N^{2}+485N-502}{48}{N\choose 5}\Psi^{N-4}(L\Psi)^{4}
for
N\geq 5
.

It is convenient to introduce some auxiliary notation. For
N\geq 1
and
1\leq j\leq N
denote

\mathcal{I}(N,j):=\bigg\{k=(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})\in\mathbb{N}^{N-j+1}\;:\;\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}k_{i}=N\;\;\textrm{and}\;\;\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}ik_{i}=N-j\bigg\},

so that the range of summation in (
3.9
) is
k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)
.
Observe that for
k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)
one has
k_{0}\geq j
. Further, we adopt standard conventions concerning multi-indices
and multiplication, for instance for
k=(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{N-j})
,
the expression
\lambda^{k}
is understood as
\lambda_{0}^{k_{0}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{N-j}^{k_{N-j}}
. Thus (
3.9
)
can be written as

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\lambda)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)}a_{N,j}(k)\,\lambda^{k}.

Finally, we denote by
e_{0},e_{1},\ldots
successive coordinate vectors and we identify multi-indices that differ only by
zeros in the last entries, e.g.
(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})\equiv(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j},0,\ldots,0)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Then
a_{N,N}(Ne_{0})=1
, and for
1\leq j\leq N+1
,
k\in\mathcal{I}(N+1,j)
one has

(3.11)

\begin{split}a_{N+1,j}(k)&=\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}\,a_{N,j-1}(k-e_{0})\\
&\quad+\chi_{\{j\neq N+1\}}\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}\chi_{\{k_{i+1}\neq 0\}}(k_{i}+\chi_{\{i\neq 0\}})\,a_{N,j}(k-e_{0}+e_{i}-e_{i+1}).\end{split}

Proof.

Let
N\geq 1
. To begin with, we observe that
a_{N,N}(Ne_{0})=1
(notice that
\mathcal{I}(N,N)=\{Ne_{0}\}
).
Indeed, by (
3.8
) we have

a_{N+1,N+1}\big((N+1)e_{0}\big)\lambda_{0}^{N+1}=\mathbf{A}_{N+1,N+1}(\lambda)=\lambda_{0}\mathbf{A}_{N,N}(\lambda)=a_{N,N}(Ne_{0})\lambda_{0}^{N+1}

and hence
a_{N,N}(Ne_{0})=a_{1,1}(e_{0})=1
.

Now fix
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. By the Leibniz rule we have

\Psi L\big((\mathcal{L}\Psi)^{k}\big)=\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}k_{i}(\mathcal{L}\Psi)^{k+e_{0}-e_{i}+e_{i+1}},

where for
k=(k_{0},\ldots,k_{N-j})
we used the notation
(\mathcal{L}\Psi)^{k}=\prod_{i=0}^{N-j}(L^{i}\Psi)^{k_{i}}
.

Hence, by (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), we obtain

(3.12)

\mathbf{A}_{N+1,j}(\lambda)=\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j-1)}a_{N,j-1}(k)\lambda^{k+e_{0}}+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)}a_{N,j}(k)\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}k_{i}\lambda^{k+e_{0}-e_{i}+e_{i+1}}.

Now the crucial observations are that

\big\{k+e_{0}:k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j-1)\big\}=\mathcal{I}(N+1,j),

and that for
0\leq i\leq N-j

\big\{k+e_{0}-e_{i}+e_{i+1}:k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j),k_{i}\neq 0\big\}=\mathcal{I}(N+1,j)\cap\{k_{i+1}\neq 0\},

which can be easily verified. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N+1,j}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N+1,j)}a_{N,j-1}(k-e_{0})\lambda^{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N+1,j)}\chi_{\{k_{i+1}\neq 0\}}(k_{i}+\chi_{\{i\neq 0\}})a_{N,j}(k-e_{0}+e_{i}-e_{i+1})\lambda^{k}.

This proves (
3.11
).
∎

Next, we evaluate
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}
at a particular point.
12
12

12

Some other evaluations of
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}
are already known, see
[
5
]
. In particular,

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(1,\ldots,1)=\left[N\atop j\right]
(unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind) and

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(1,1,0,\ldots,0)=\left\{N\atop j\right\}
(Stirling numbers of the second kind).
The numbers appearing on the right-hand side of (
3.13
) are coefficients of Bessel polynomials of degree
N-1

multiplying there monomials of degree
N-j
, see
[
36
, A001498]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
1\leq j\leq N
. Then

(3.13)

\mathbf{A}_{N,j}\big(0!,1!,\ldots,(N-j)!\big)=\frac{(2N-j-1)!}{2^{N-j}(N-j)!(j-1)!}.

Proof.

For
j=N
, and in particular when
N=1
, the lemma holds since
\mathbf{A}_{N,N}(\lambda)=\lambda_{0}^{N}
. Otherwise, we use induction on
N
.
For the inductive step assume that
1\leq j\leq N
and observe that by (
3.12
) one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N+1,j}(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{N+1-j})

\displaystyle=\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}\lambda_{0}\mathbf{A}_{N,j-1}(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{N+1-j})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)}a_{N,j}(k)\Bigg(\prod_{l=0}^{N-j}\lambda_{l}^{k_{l}}\Bigg)\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}\frac{k_{i}\lambda_{0}\lambda_{i+1}}{\lambda_{i}}.

Thus, by the inductive hypothesis and the identity

\sum_{i=0}^{N-j}(i+1)k_{i}=2N-j,\qquad k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j),

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N+1,j}\big(0!,\ldots,(N+1-j)!\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}(2N-j)!}{2^{N+1-j}(N+1-j)!(j-2)!}+(2N-j)\frac{(2N-j-1)!}{2^{N-j}(N-j)!(j-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2N-j)!\big[\chi_{\{j\neq 1\}}(j-1)+2(N-j+1)\big]}{2^{N+1-j}(N+1-j)!(j-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2(N+1)-j-1)!}{2^{N+1-j}(N+1-j)!(j-1)!}.

The last expression is the right-hand side in (
3.13
) with
N
replaced by
N+1
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In order to estimate
\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)
from above we shall need upper bounds for
L^{i}\Psi(\varphi)
, which we now establish.
The key observation here is the series expansion (which follows from the well known expansion of the cosecant function,
see e.g.
[
6
, pp. 88–89]
)

(3.14)

\Psi(\varphi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}(2^{2n}-2)B_{2n}}{(2n)!}\varphi^{2n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4(2^{2n-1}-1)\zeta(2n)}{(2\pi)^{2n}}\varphi^{2n},\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi).

Here
B_{2n}
are Bernoulli numbers and
\zeta
is the Riemann zeta function; note that
\zeta(0)=-1/2
.
The expansion in (
3.14
) has strictly positive coefficients, and clearly
L^{i}\Psi
is of the same form,
again with strictly positive coefficients. So
L^{i}\Psi
,
i=0,\ldots,N-1
, are increasing on
[0,\pi)
.
The maxima
(L^{i}\Psi)(\pi/2)
over
[0,\pi/2]
can be quite precisely estimated from the positive series.
13
13

13

The main bound of Lemma
3.4
is quite precise except for several initial
i

(the accuracy improves with
i
growing). For those
i
, however, it is straightforward to compute exact values of

L^{i}\Psi(\pi/2)
. One has
\Psi(\pi/2)=\pi/2\approx 1.570796\;[\approx 2.666667]
,

(L\Psi)(\pi/2)=2/\pi\approx 0.636620\;[\approx 0.720506]
,

(L^{2}\Psi)(\pi/2)=2/\pi-8/\pi^{3}\approx 0.378607\;[\approx 0.389347]
and

(L^{3}\Psi)(\pi/2)=96/\pi^{5}\approx 0.313705\;[\approx 0.315593]
,
where in square brackets the corresponding approximations of the upper bound from Lemma
3.4
are given.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

0<L^{i}\Psi(\varphi)\leq L^{i}\Psi\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)<\frac{8^{i+1}i!}{3^{i+1}\pi^{2i}},\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi/2].

Proof.

We need to prove only the last inequality.
For
i=0
the inequality is trivial so assume that
i\geq 1
. By (
3.14
) and the series representation of the zeta function we have

\displaystyle L^{i}\Psi\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=i}^{\infty}\frac{4(2^{2n-1}-1)\zeta(2n)2n(2n-2)\cdot\ldots\cdot(2n-2i+2)}{(2\pi)^{2n}}\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)^{2n-2i}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-2i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4(2^{2n+2i-1}-1)\zeta(2n+2i)}{4^{2n}}\,\frac{(2n+2i)!!}{(2n)!!}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{2}{\pi}\Big)^{2i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{2}{2^{2n+2i}}-\frac{4}{4^{2n+2i}}\Big)\frac{\zeta(2n+2i)(2n+2i)!!}{(2n)!!}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{2}{\pi}\Big)^{2i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{2}{(2k)^{2n+2i}}-\frac{4}{(4k)^{2n+2i}}\bigg)\frac{(2n+2i)!!}{(2n)!!}

\displaystyle=2\Big(\frac{2}{\pi}\Big)^{2i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n+2i)!!}{(2n)!!(2k)^{2n+2i}},

where in the last equality we used the fact that for an absolutely convergent series

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}
one has
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(b_{k}-2b_{2k})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}b_{k}
.
Using induction it is easy to check that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n+2i)!!}{(2n)!!}x^{2n}=\frac{2^{i}i!}{(1-x^{2})^{i+1}},\qquad x\in(-1,1).

Thus we arrive at the identity

L^{i}\Psi\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)=8i!\Big(\frac{8}{\pi^{2}}\Big)^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{k^{2}}{(4k^{2}-1)^{i+1}}.

Now we finish the proof by observing that the above alternating series is strictly bounded from above by the first term
which equals
3^{-(i+1)}
.
∎

We are now in a position to estimate
\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
1\leq j\leq N
. Then

0<\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)<\Big(\frac{8}{3}\Big)^{N}\Big(\frac{4}{3\pi^{2}}\Big)^{N-j}\Big(\frac{3\pi}{16}\Big)^{j}\frac{(2N-j-1)!}{(N-j)!(j-1)!},\qquad\varphi\in[0,\pi/2].

Proof.

By strict positivity of
a_{N,j}(k)
,
k\in\mathcal{I}(N,j)
, the polynomial
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\lambda)
is strictly increasing
in each variable
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
,
i=0,\ldots,N-j
. Since
(L^{i}\Psi)(\varphi)
,
i=0,1,2,\ldots
, are strictly positive
for
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]
, it follows that
\Phi_{N,j}>0
on
[0,\pi/2]
. Moreover, we can estimate
\Phi_{N,j}
on

[0,\pi/2]
from above just by estimating
\mathbf{A}_{N,j}(\lambda)
, where for
\lambda_{i}
we insert the upper bounds for

L^{i}\Psi
on
[0,\pi/2]
established in Lemma
3.4
(for
i=0
we use the better bound
\Psi(\varphi)\leq\Psi(\pi/2)=\pi/2
).
This gives

\displaystyle\Phi_{N,j}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{A}_{N,j}\bigg(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{8^{1+1}\cdot 1!}{3^{1+1}\pi^{2\cdot 1}},\ldots,\frac{8^{N-j+1}(N-j)!}{3^{N-j+1}\pi^{2(N-j)}}\bigg)
